# Vezafra: reference manual
# Vezafra reference manual

Vezafra is a small imperative language. A program is a list of statements. The grader calls the function `budru solve(...)` with arguments and prints the value it returns.

## Words and values
- Function definition: `budru`. Conditionals: `glugi` / `supine`. Loops: `botu`. Return: `kezvai`.
- Booleans: `vedreebe` and `kreedena`. Integers: `0`, `42` (negative numbers use the prefix negation operator `-`). Strings: double quotes, e.g. "hi".
- Lists are written `[a, b, c]`. Values are immutable: `append` returns a new list.
- A function that does not return a value yields `none`.
- Printed values: integers as digits, booleans as `true`/`false`, strings as-is, lists as `[a, b]`. Strings inside a list are in double quotes, e.g. `["a", 1]`.

## Statements
- Assignment: `name = expression`. The first assignment inside a function creates the variable.
- Blocks start with `poutrai` and end with `teevu`. Statements inside a block follow the same rules.
- Statement endings: each simple statement ends with `.`.
- Identifiers are plain letters, digits and underscores (not starting with a digit).

## Operators and precedence
- Negation: `-x`. Logical not: `!x`. Both bind tighter than any binary operator.
- Binary operators, lowest binding first (same line = same precedence, grouped left to right):
1. `||`
2. `+`, `-`
3. `&&`
4. `==`, `!=`, `<`, `<=`, `>`, `>=`
5. `*`, `/`, `%`
- Calls and indexing bind tightest of all.
- Parentheses group as usual.

## Calls
- A call is written `name(arg, ...)`. Built-in and user-defined functions use the same form.

## Indexing
- `xs[i]` reads an element. Indices start at **0**: the first element is `xs[0]`, the last is `xs[0 + len(xs) - 1]`.
- Indexing outside the valid range is a runtime error.

## Truthiness
- `kreedena`, `vedreebe`: as written. `none`: false.
- The integer 0 is false. An empty list or empty string is false. Any other integer is true.

## Integer arithmetic
- `/` on integers rounds toward zero: `-7 / 2` evaluates to -3.
- `%` takes the sign of the divisor: `-7 % 3` evaluates to 2, and `7 % -3` evaluates to -2.
- Division or modulo by zero is a runtime error. `+` also joins two strings.

## and / or
- `and` and `or` short-circuit. They return a boolean: `0 or 5` evaluates to true, `vedreebe and 0` evaluates to false.

## Built-in functions
- `brupu(x)`: number of items in a list or characters in a string.
- `trimai(xs, v)`: a new list with `v` added at the end.
- `briree(x)`: the printed form of `x` as a string.
- `kepen(x)`: prints `x` on its own line.

## Limits
- Programs get a bounded number of steps, a bounded call depth and bounded list and string sizes. Exceeding a limit (for example an infinite loop) is an error.

## Worked examples (executed when this manual was generated)

### Integer division and modulo
```

kepen(- 7 % 3) .
kepen(7 / - 2) .
kepen(7 % - 3) .
```
Output:
```
-3
2
-3
-2
```

### Operator precedence
```

kepen(1 == (0 + 1)) .
```
Output:
```

true
```

### Truthiness in conditions
```

  kepen("zero is true") .
teevu supine poutrai
  kepen("zero is false") .
teevu

  kepen("empty list is true") .
teevu supine poutrai
  kepen("empty list is false") .
teevu
```
Output:
```
zero is false
empty list is false
```

### Indexing
```

kepen(xs[2]) .
```
Output:
```

30
```

### and / or
```

```
Output:
```

```

### Functions, loops and calls
```

  kezvai a + b .
teevu

i = 1 .
```

```

5
```

# Task: bsearch
Define solve(xs, t). xs is a list of distinct integers sorted ascending. Return the 0-based position of t in xs, or -1 if t is absent. The answer is always 0-based, whatever the language's index base.

## Visible examples
- solve([1, 3, 5, 7], 5) returns 2
- solve([1, 3, 5, 7], 1) returns 0

## Answer format
Define a function named `solve` with the parameters described above. Reply with one fenced code block containing the complete program (top-level statements are allowed). Do not explain.

Attempt 1: failed, 0/7 tests, 0 in / 0 out tokens
Model answer
```python
def solve(xs, t):
    lo = 0
    hi = (len(xs) - 1)
    while (lo <= hi):
        mid = ((lo + hi) // 2)
        x = xs[mid]
        if (x == t):
            return mid
        if (x < t):
            lo = (mid + 1)
        else:
            hi = (mid - 1)
    return (-1)
```

Prompt (attempt 1)

# Plakri: reference manual
# Plakri reference manual

Plakri is a small imperative language. A program is a list of statements. The grader calls the function `pegun solve(...)` with arguments and prints the value it returns.

## Words and values
- Function definition: `pegun`. Conditionals: `krivoulo` / `ribrumee`. Loops: `skedre`. Return: `gamee`.
- Booleans: `krufeebru` and `treplaigee`. Integers: `0`, `42` (negative numbers use the prefix negation operator `~`). Strings: double quotes, e.g. "hi".
- Lists are written `[a, b, c]`. Values are immutable: `append` returns a new list.
- A function that does not return a value yields `none`.
- Printed values: integers as digits, booleans as `true`/`false`, strings as-is, lists as `[a, b]`. Strings inside a list are in double quotes, e.g. `["a", 1]`.

## Statements
- Assignment: `name := expression`. The first assignment inside a function creates the variable.
- Blocks start with `meesete` and end with `buskee`. Statements inside a block follow the same rules.
- Statement endings: each simple statement ends with `$`.
- Identifiers are plain letters, digits and underscores (not starting with a digit).

## Operators and precedence
- Negation: `~x`. Logical not: `!x`. Both bind tighter than any binary operator.
- Binary operators, lowest binding first (same line = same precedence, grouped left to right):
1. `|`
2. `+`, `-`
3. `*`, `/`, `%`
4. `&`
5. `===`, `/=`, `<`, `=<`, `>`, `>=`
- Calls and indexing bind tightest of all.
- Parentheses group as usual.

## Calls
- A call is written `(arg, ...) name`, with the arguments first and the name last. Built-in and user-defined functions use the same form.

## Indexing
- `xs[i]` reads an element. Indices start at **1**: the first element is `xs[1]`, the last is `xs[1 + len(xs) - 1]`.
- Indexing outside the valid range is a runtime error.

## Truthiness
- `treplaigee`, `krufeebru`: as written. `none`: false.
- The integer 0 is false. An empty list or empty string is true. Any other integer is true.

## Integer arithmetic
- `/` on integers rounds toward negative infinity: `-7 / 2` evaluates to -4.
- `%` takes the sign of the divisor: `-7 % 3` evaluates to 2, and `7 % -3` evaluates to -2.
- Division or modulo by zero is a runtime error. `+` also joins two strings.

## and / or
- `and` and `or` short-circuit. They return the operand that decided the result: `0 or 5` evaluates to 5, `krufeebru and 0` evaluates to 0.

## Built-in functions
- `vopiro(x)`: number of items in a list or characters in a string.
- `zvuzukra(xs, v)`: a new list with `v` added at the end.
- `trudou(x)`: the printed form of `x` as a string.
- `saiskee(x)`: prints `x` on its own line.

## Limits
- Programs get a bounded number of steps, a bounded call depth and bounded list and string sizes. Exceeding a limit (for example an infinite loop) is an error.

## Worked examples (executed when this manual was generated)

### Integer division and modulo
```

(~ 7 % 3) saiskee $
(7 / ~ 2) saiskee $
(7 % ~ 3) saiskee $
```

```

  ("empty list is false") saiskee $
```

# Veleeni: reference manual
# Veleeni reference manual

Veleeni is a small imperative language. A program is a list of statements. The grader calls the function `romazva solve(...)` with arguments and prints the value it returns.

## Words and values
- Function definition: `romazva`. Conditionals: `kouzvi` / `traikeru`. Loops: `soureekra`. Return: `kromee`.
- Booleans: `pezvimo` and `plukrafee`. Integers: `0`, `42` (negative numbers use the prefix negation operator `-`). Strings: double quotes, e.g. "hi".
- Lists are written `[a, b, c]`. Values are immutable: `append` returns a new list.
- A function that does not return a value yields `none`.
- Printed values: integers as digits, booleans as `true`/`false`, strings as-is, lists as `[a, b]`. Strings inside a list are in double quotes, e.g. `["a", 1]`.

## Statements
- Assignment: `name = expression`. The first assignment inside a function creates the variable.
- Blocks start with `{` and end with `}`. Statements inside a block follow the same rules.
- Statement endings: a statement ends at the end of its line.
- In a newline-ended language a block opener must be on the same line as its statement, and `traikeru` must be on the same line as the `}` that closes the `if` block.
- Identifiers are plain letters, digits and underscores (not starting with a digit).

## Operators and precedence
- Negation: `-x`. Logical not: `fudaix`. Both bind tighter than any binary operator.
- Binary operators, lowest binding first (same line = same precedence, grouped left to right):
1. `||`
2. `&`
3. `===`, `!=`, `<`, `=<`, `>`, `=>`
4. `+`, `-`
5. `*`, `/`, `%`
- Calls and indexing bind tightest of all.
- Parentheses group as usual.

## Calls
- A call is written `(arg, ...) name`, with the arguments first and the name last. Built-in and user-defined functions use the same form.

## Indexing
- `xs[i]` reads an element. Indices start at **0**: the first element is `xs[0]`, the last is `xs[0 + len(xs) - 1]`.
- Indexing outside the valid range is a runtime error.

## Truthiness
- `plukrafee`, `pezvimo`: as written. `none`: false.
- The integer 0 is true. An empty list or empty string is false. Any other integer is true.

## Integer arithmetic
- `/` on integers rounds toward negative infinity: `-7 / 2` evaluates to -4.
- `%` takes the sign of the dividend: `-7 % 3` evaluates to -1, and `7 % -3` evaluates to 1.
- Division or modulo by zero is a runtime error. `+` also joins two strings.

## and / or
- `and` and `or` short-circuit. They return the operand that decided the result: `0 or 5` evaluates to 0, `pezvimo and 0` evaluates to 0.

## Built-in functions
- `kraizo(x)`: number of items in a list or characters in a string.
- `brale(xs, v)`: a new list with `v` added at the end.
- `dikuki(x)`: the printed form of `x` as a string.
- `mutre(x)`: prints `x` on its own line.

## Limits
- Programs get a bounded number of steps, a bounded call depth and bounded list and string sizes. Exceeding a limit (for example an infinite loop) is an error.

## Worked examples (executed when this manual was generated)

### Integer division and modulo
```
(- 7 / 2) mutre
(- 7 % 3) mutre
(7 / - 2) mutre
(7 % - 3) mutre
```
Output:
```
-4
-1
-4
1
```

### Operator precedence
```
(2 + 3 * 4) mutre
(1 === 0 + 1) mutre
```
Output:
```
14
true
```

### Truthiness in conditions
```
kouzvi 0 {
  ("zero is true") mutre
} traikeru {
  ("zero is false") mutre
}
kouzvi[] {
  ("empty list is true") mutre
} traikeru {
  ("empty list is false") mutre
}
```
Output:
```
zero is true
empty list is false
```

### Indexing
```
xs = [10, 20, 30]
(xs[0]) mutre
(xs[2]) mutre
```
Output:
```
10
30
```

### and / or
```
(0 || 5) mutre
(pezvimo & 0) mutre
```
Output:
```
0
0
```

### Functions, loops and calls
```
romazva add(a, b) {
  kromee a + b
}
acc = 0
i = 1
soureekra i =< 3 {
  acc = acc + i
  i = i + 1
}
(acc) mutre
((2, 3) add) mutre
```
Output:
```
6
5
```

# Task: bsearch
Define solve(xs, t). xs is a list of distinct integers sorted ascending. Return the 0-based position of t in xs, or -1 if t is absent. The answer is always 0-based, whatever the language's index base.

## Visible examples
- solve([1, 3, 5, 7], 5) returns 2
- solve([1, 3, 5, 7], 1) returns 0

## Answer format
Define a function named `solve` with the parameters described above. Reply with one fenced code block containing the complete program (top-level statements are allowed). Do not explain.

Attempt 1: failed, 0/7 tests, 0 in / 0 out tokens
Model answer
```python
def solve(xs, t):
    lo = 0
    hi = (len(xs) - 1)
    while (lo <= hi):
        mid = ((lo + hi) // 2)
        x = xs[mid]
        if (x == t):
            return mid
        if (x < t):
            lo = (mid + 1)
        else:
            hi = (mid - 1)
    return (-1)
```
Grader feedback
Your program has a syntax error: line 1: unexpected character ':'
PASS write.bsearch · Veleeni (seed 3) · oracle · 1 attempt(s)
Prompt (attempt 1)

# Task: bsearch
Define solve(xs, t). xs is a list of distinct integers sorted ascending. Return the 0-based position of t in xs, or -1 if t is absent. The answer is always 0-based, whatever the language's index base.

## Visible examples
- solve([1, 3, 5, 7], 5) returns 2
- solve([1, 3, 5, 7], 1) returns 0

## Answer format
Define a function named `solve` with the parameters described above. Reply with one fenced code block containing the complete program (top-level statements are allowed). Do not explain.

Attempt 1: passed, 7/7 tests, 0 in / 0 out tokens
Model answer
```
romazva solve(xs, t) {
  lo = 0
  hi = (xs) kraizo - 1
  soureekra lo =< hi {
    mid = (lo + hi) / 2
    x = xs[mid]
    kouzvi x === t {
      kromee mid
    }
    kouzvi x < t {
      lo = mid + 1
    } traikeru {
      hi = mid - 1
    }
  }
  kromee - 1
}
```

# Vezafra: reference manual
# Vezafra reference manual

Vezafra is a small imperative language. A program is a list of statements. The grader calls the function `budru solve(...)` with arguments and prints the value it returns.

## Words and values
- Function definition: `budru`. Conditionals: `glugi` / `supine`. Loops: `botu`. Return: `kezvai`.
- Booleans: `vedreebe` and `kreedena`. Integers: `0`, `42` (negative numbers use the prefix negation operator `-`). Strings: double quotes, e.g. "hi".
- Lists are written `[a, b, c]`. Values are immutable: `append` returns a new list.
- A function that does not return a value yields `none`.
- Printed values: integers as digits, booleans as `true`/`false`, strings as-is, lists as `[a, b]`. Strings inside a list are in double quotes, e.g. `["a", 1]`.

## Statements
- Assignment: `name = expression`. The first assignment inside a function creates the variable.
- Blocks start with `poutrai` and end with `teevu`. Statements inside a block follow the same rules.
- Statement endings: each simple statement ends with `.`.
- Identifiers are plain letters, digits and underscores (not starting with a digit).

## Operators and precedence
- Negation: `-x`. Logical not: `!x`. Both bind tighter than any binary operator.
- Binary operators, lowest binding first (same line = same precedence, grouped left to right):
1. `||`
2. `+`, `-`
3. `&&`
4. `==`, `!=`, `<`, `<=`, `>`, `>=`
5. `*`, `/`, `%`
- Calls and indexing bind tightest of all.
- Parentheses group as usual.

## Calls
- A call is written `name(arg, ...)`. Built-in and user-defined functions use the same form.

## Indexing
- `xs[i]` reads an element. Indices start at **0**: the first element is `xs[0]`, the last is `xs[0 + len(xs) - 1]`.
- Indexing outside the valid range is a runtime error.

## Truthiness
- `kreedena`, `vedreebe`: as written. `none`: false.
- The integer 0 is false. An empty list or empty string is false. Any other integer is true.

## Integer arithmetic
- `/` on integers rounds toward zero: `-7 / 2` evaluates to -3.
- `%` takes the sign of the divisor: `-7 % 3` evaluates to 2, and `7 % -3` evaluates to -2.
- Division or modulo by zero is a runtime error. `+` also joins two strings.

## and / or
- `and` and `or` short-circuit. They return a boolean: `0 or 5` evaluates to true, `vedreebe and 0` evaluates to false.

## Built-in functions
- `brupu(x)`: number of items in a list or characters in a string.
- `trimai(xs, v)`: a new list with `v` added at the end.
- `briree(x)`: the printed form of `x` as a string.
- `kepen(x)`: prints `x` on its own line.

## Limits
- Programs get a bounded number of steps, a bounded call depth and bounded list and string sizes. Exceeding a limit (for example an infinite loop) is an error.

## Worked examples (executed when this manual was generated)

### Integer division and modulo
```
kepen(- 7 / 2) .
kepen(- 7 % 3) .
kepen(7 / - 2) .
kepen(7 % - 3) .
```
Output:
```
-3
2
-3
-2
```

### Operator precedence
```
kepen(2 + 3 * 4) .
kepen(1 == (0 + 1)) .
```
Output:
```
14
true
```

### Truthiness in conditions
```
glugi 0 poutrai
  kepen("zero is true") .
teevu supine poutrai
  kepen("zero is false") .
teevu
glugi[] poutrai
  kepen("empty list is true") .
teevu supine poutrai
  kepen("empty list is false") .
teevu
```
Output:
```
zero is false
empty list is false
```

### Indexing
```
xs = [10, 20, 30] .
kepen(xs[0]) .
kepen(xs[2]) .
```
Output:
```
10
30
```

### and / or
```
kepen(0 || 5) .
kepen(vedreebe && 0) .
```
Output:
```
true
false
```

### Functions, loops and calls
```
budru add(a, b) poutrai
  kezvai a + b .
teevu
acc = 0 .
i = 1 .
botu i <= 3 poutrai
  acc = acc + i .
  i = i + 1 .
teevu
kepen(acc) .
kepen(add(2, 3)) .
```
Output:
```
6
5
```

# Task: count digits
Define solve(n) returning how many decimal digits the non-negative integer n has (0 has one digit).

## Visible examples
- solve(7) returns 1
- solve(10) returns 2

## Answer format
Define a function named `solve` with the parameters described above. Reply with one fenced code block containing the complete program (top-level statements are allowed). Do not explain.

Attempt 1: failed, 0/6 tests, 0 in / 0 out tokens
Model answer
```python
def solve(n):
    count = 1
    m = n
    while (m >= 10):
        m = (m // 10)
        count = (count + 1)
    return count
```
Grader feedback
Your program has a syntax error: line 1: unexpected character ':'
PASS write.count_digits · Vezafra (seed 1) · oracle · 1 attempt(s)
Prompt (attempt 1)

# Plakri: reference manual
# Plakri reference manual

Plakri is a small imperative language. A program is a list of statements. The grader calls the function `pegun solve(...)` with arguments and prints the value it returns.

## Words and values
- Function definition: `pegun`. Conditionals: `krivoulo` / `ribrumee`. Loops: `skedre`. Return: `gamee`.
- Booleans: `krufeebru` and `treplaigee`. Integers: `0`, `42` (negative numbers use the prefix negation operator `~`). Strings: double quotes, e.g. "hi".
- Lists are written `[a, b, c]`. Values are immutable: `append` returns a new list.
- A function that does not return a value yields `none`.
- Printed values: integers as digits, booleans as `true`/`false`, strings as-is, lists as `[a, b]`. Strings inside a list are in double quotes, e.g. `["a", 1]`.

## Statements
- Assignment: `name := expression`. The first assignment inside a function creates the variable.
- Blocks start with `meesete` and end with `buskee`. Statements inside a block follow the same rules.
- Statement endings: each simple statement ends with `$`.
- Identifiers are plain letters, digits and underscores (not starting with a digit).

## Operators and precedence
- Negation: `~x`. Logical not: `!x`. Both bind tighter than any binary operator.
- Binary operators, lowest binding first (same line = same precedence, grouped left to right):
1. `|`
2. `+`, `-`
3. `*`, `/`, `%`
4. `&`
5. `===`, `/=`, `<`, `=<`, `>`, `>=`
- Calls and indexing bind tightest of all.
- Parentheses group as usual.

## Calls
- A call is written `(arg, ...) name`, with the arguments first and the name last. Built-in and user-defined functions use the same form.

## Indexing
- `xs[i]` reads an element. Indices start at **1**: the first element is `xs[1]`, the last is `xs[1 + len(xs) - 1]`.
- Indexing outside the valid range is a runtime error.

## Truthiness
- `treplaigee`, `krufeebru`: as written. `none`: false.
- The integer 0 is false. An empty list or empty string is true. Any other integer is true.

## Integer arithmetic
- `/` on integers rounds toward negative infinity: `-7 / 2` evaluates to -4.
- `%` takes the sign of the divisor: `-7 % 3` evaluates to 2, and `7 % -3` evaluates to -2.
- Division or modulo by zero is a runtime error. `+` also joins two strings.

## and / or
- `and` and `or` short-circuit. They return the operand that decided the result: `0 or 5` evaluates to 5, `krufeebru and 0` evaluates to 0.

## Built-in functions
- `vopiro(x)`: number of items in a list or characters in a string.
- `zvuzukra(xs, v)`: a new list with `v` added at the end.
- `trudou(x)`: the printed form of `x` as a string.
- `saiskee(x)`: prints `x` on its own line.

## Limits
- Programs get a bounded number of steps, a bounded call depth and bounded list and string sizes. Exceeding a limit (for example an infinite loop) is an error.

## Worked examples (executed when this manual was generated)

### Integer division and modulo
```
(~ 7 / 2) saiskee $
(~ 7 % 3) saiskee $
(7 / ~ 2) saiskee $
(7 % ~ 3) saiskee $
```
Output:
```
-4
2
-4
-2
```

### Operator precedence
```
(2 + 3 * 4) saiskee $
(1 === (0 + 1)) saiskee $
```
Output:
```
14
true
```

### Truthiness in conditions
```
krivoulo 0 meesete
  ("zero is true") saiskee $
buskee ribrumee meesete
  ("zero is false") saiskee $
buskee
krivoulo[] meesete
  ("empty list is true") saiskee $
buskee ribrumee meesete
  ("empty list is false") saiskee $
buskee
```
Output:
```
zero is false
empty list is true
```

### Indexing
```
xs := [10, 20, 30] $
(xs[1]) saiskee $
(xs[3]) saiskee $
```
Output:
```
10
30
```

### and / or
```
(0 | 5) saiskee $
(krufeebru & 0) saiskee $
```
Output:
```
5
0
```

### Functions, loops and calls
```
pegun add(a, b) meesete
  gamee a + b $
buskee
acc := 0 $
i := 1 $
skedre i =< 3 meesete
  acc := acc + i $
  i := i + 1 $
buskee
(acc) saiskee $
((2, 3) add) saiskee $
```
Output:
```
6
5
```

# Plakri: reference manual
# Plakri reference manual

Plakri is a small imperative language. A program is a list of statements. The grader calls the function `pegun solve(...)` with arguments and prints the value it returns.

## Words and values
- Function definition: `pegun`. Conditionals: `krivoulo` / `ribrumee`. Loops: `skedre`. Return: `gamee`.
- Booleans: `krufeebru` and `treplaigee`. Integers: `0`, `42` (negative numbers use the prefix negation operator `~`). Strings: double quotes, e.g. "hi".
- Lists are written `[a, b, c]`. Values are immutable: `append` returns a new list.
- A function that does not return a value yields `none`.
- Printed values: integers as digits, booleans as `true`/`false`, strings as-is, lists as `[a, b]`. Strings inside a list are in double quotes, e.g. `["a", 1]`.

## Statements
- Assignment: `name := expression`. The first assignment inside a function creates the variable.
- Blocks start with `meesete` and end with `buskee`. Statements inside a block follow the same rules.
- Statement endings: each simple statement ends with `$`.
- Identifiers are plain letters, digits and underscores (not starting with a digit).

## Operators and precedence
- Negation: `~x`. Logical not: `!x`. Both bind tighter than any binary operator.
- Binary operators, lowest binding first (same line = same precedence, grouped left to right):
1. `|`
2. `+`, `-`
3. `*`, `/`, `%`
4. `&`
5. `===`, `/=`, `<`, `=<`, `>`, `>=`
- Calls and indexing bind tightest of all.
- Parentheses group as usual.

## Calls
- A call is written `(arg, ...) name`, with the arguments first and the name last. Built-in and user-defined functions use the same form.

## Indexing
- `xs[i]` reads an element. Indices start at **1**: the first element is `xs[1]`, the last is `xs[1 + len(xs) - 1]`.
- Indexing outside the valid range is a runtime error.

## Truthiness
- `treplaigee`, `krufeebru`: as written. `none`: false.
- The integer 0 is false. An empty list or empty string is true. Any other integer is true.

## Integer arithmetic
- `/` on integers rounds toward negative infinity: `-7 / 2` evaluates to -4.
- `%` takes the sign of the divisor: `-7 % 3` evaluates to 2, and `7 % -3` evaluates to -2.
- Division or modulo by zero is a runtime error. `+` also joins two strings.

## and / or
- `and` and `or` short-circuit. They return the operand that decided the result: `0 or 5` evaluates to 5, `krufeebru and 0` evaluates to 0.

## Built-in functions
- `vopiro(x)`: number of items in a list or characters in a string.
- `zvuzukra(xs, v)`: a new list with `v` added at the end.
- `trudou(x)`: the printed form of `x` as a string.
- `saiskee(x)`: prints `x` on its own line.

## Limits
- Programs get a bounded number of steps, a bounded call depth and bounded list and string sizes. Exceeding a limit (for example an infinite loop) is an error.

## Worked examples (executed when this manual was generated)

### Integer division and modulo
```
(~ 7 / 2) saiskee $
(~ 7 % 3) saiskee $
(7 / ~ 2) saiskee $
(7 % ~ 3) saiskee $
```
Output:
```
-4
2
-4
-2
```

### Operator precedence
```
(2 + 3 * 4) saiskee $
(1 === (0 + 1)) saiskee $
```
Output:
```
14
true
```

### Truthiness in conditions
```
krivoulo 0 meesete
  ("zero is true") saiskee $
buskee ribrumee meesete
  ("zero is false") saiskee $
buskee
krivoulo[] meesete
  ("empty list is true") saiskee $
buskee ribrumee meesete
  ("empty list is false") saiskee $
buskee
```
Output:
```
zero is false
empty list is true
```

### Indexing
```
xs := [10, 20, 30] $
(xs[1]) saiskee $
(xs[3]) saiskee $
```
Output:
```
10
30
```

### and / or
```
(0 | 5) saiskee $
(krufeebru & 0) saiskee $
```
Output:
```
5
0
```

### Functions, loops and calls
```
pegun add(a, b) meesete
  gamee a + b $
buskee
acc := 0 $
i := 1 $
skedre i =< 3 meesete
  acc := acc + i $
  i := i + 1 $
buskee
(acc) saiskee $
((2, 3) add) saiskee $
```
Output:
```
6
5
```

# Veleeni: reference manual
# Veleeni reference manual

Veleeni is a small imperative language. A program is a list of statements. The grader calls the function `romazva solve(...)` with arguments and prints the value it returns.

## Words and values
- Function definition: `romazva`. Conditionals: `kouzvi` / `traikeru`. Loops: `soureekra`. Return: `kromee`.
- Booleans: `pezvimo` and `plukrafee`. Integers: `0`, `42` (negative numbers use the prefix negation operator `-`). Strings: double quotes, e.g. "hi".
- Lists are written `[a, b, c]`. Values are immutable: `append` returns a new list.
- A function that does not return a value yields `none`.
- Printed values: integers as digits, booleans as `true`/`false`, strings as-is, lists as `[a, b]`. Strings inside a list are in double quotes, e.g. `["a", 1]`.

## Statements
- Assignment: `name = expression`. The first assignment inside a function creates the variable.
- Blocks start with `{` and end with `}`. Statements inside a block follow the same rules.
- Statement endings: a statement ends at the end of its line.
- In a newline-ended language a block opener must be on the same line as its statement, and `traikeru` must be on the same line as the `}` that closes the `if` block.
- Identifiers are plain letters, digits and underscores (not starting with a digit).

## Operators and precedence
- Negation: `-x`. Logical not: `fudaix`. Both bind tighter than any binary operator.
- Binary operators, lowest binding first (same line = same precedence, grouped left to right):
1. `||`
2. `&`
3. `===`, `!=`, `<`, `=<`, `>`, `=>`
4. `+`, `-`
5. `*`, `/`, `%`
- Calls and indexing bind tightest of all.
- Parentheses group as usual.

## Calls
- A call is written `(arg, ...) name`, with the arguments first and the name last. Built-in and user-defined functions use the same form.

## Indexing
- `xs[i]` reads an element. Indices start at **0**: the first element is `xs[0]`, the last is `xs[0 + len(xs) - 1]`.
- Indexing outside the valid range is a runtime error.

## Truthiness
- `plukrafee`, `pezvimo`: as written. `none`: false.
- The integer 0 is true. An empty list or empty string is false. Any other integer is true.

## Integer arithmetic
- `/` on integers rounds toward negative infinity: `-7 / 2` evaluates to -4.
- `%` takes the sign of the dividend: `-7 % 3` evaluates to -1, and `7 % -3` evaluates to 1.
- Division or modulo by zero is a runtime error. `+` also joins two strings.

## and / or
- `and` and `or` short-circuit. They return the operand that decided the result: `0 or 5` evaluates to 0, `pezvimo and 0` evaluates to 0.

## Built-in functions
- `kraizo(x)`: number of items in a list or characters in a string.
- `brale(xs, v)`: a new list with `v` added at the end.
- `dikuki(x)`: the printed form of `x` as a string.
- `mutre(x)`: prints `x` on its own line.

## Limits
- Programs get a bounded number of steps, a bounded call depth and bounded list and string sizes. Exceeding a limit (for example an infinite loop) is an error.

## Worked examples (executed when this manual was generated)

### Integer division and modulo
```
(- 7 / 2) mutre
(- 7 % 3) mutre
(7 / - 2) mutre
(7 % - 3) mutre
```
Output:
```
-4
-1
-4
1
```

### Operator precedence
```
(2 + 3 * 4) mutre
(1 === 0 + 1) mutre
```
Output:
```
14
true
```

### Truthiness in conditions
```
kouzvi 0 {
  ("zero is true") mutre
} traikeru {
  ("zero is false") mutre
}
kouzvi[] {
  ("empty list is true") mutre
} traikeru {
  ("empty list is false") mutre
}
```
Output:
```
zero is true
empty list is false
```

### Indexing
```
xs = [10, 20, 30]
(xs[0]) mutre
(xs[2]) mutre
```
Output:
```
10
30
```

### and / or
```
(0 || 5) mutre
(pezvimo & 0) mutre
```
Output:
```
0
0
```

### Functions, loops and calls
```
romazva add(a, b) {
  kromee a + b
}
acc = 0
i = 1
soureekra i =< 3 {
  acc = acc + i
  i = i + 1
}
(acc) mutre
((2, 3) add) mutre
```
Output:
```
6
5
```

# Vezafra: reference manual
# Vezafra reference manual

Vezafra is a small imperative language. A program is a list of statements. The grader calls the function `budru solve(...)` with arguments and prints the value it returns.

## Words and values
- Function definition: `budru`. Conditionals: `glugi` / `supine`. Loops: `botu`. Return: `kezvai`.
- Booleans: `vedreebe` and `kreedena`. Integers: `0`, `42` (negative numbers use the prefix negation operator `-`). Strings: double quotes, e.g. "hi".
- Lists are written `[a, b, c]`. Values are immutable: `append` returns a new list.
- A function that does not return a value yields `none`.
- Printed values: integers as digits, booleans as `true`/`false`, strings as-is, lists as `[a, b]`. Strings inside a list are in double quotes, e.g. `["a", 1]`.

## Statements
- Assignment: `name = expression`. The first assignment inside a function creates the variable.
- Blocks start with `poutrai` and end with `teevu`. Statements inside a block follow the same rules.
- Statement endings: each simple statement ends with `.`.
- Identifiers are plain letters, digits and underscores (not starting with a digit).

## Operators and precedence
- Negation: `-x`. Logical not: `!x`. Both bind tighter than any binary operator.
- Binary operators, lowest binding first (same line = same precedence, grouped left to right):
1. `||`
2. `+`, `-`
3. `&&`
4. `==`, `!=`, `<`, `<=`, `>`, `>=`
5. `*`, `/`, `%`
- Calls and indexing bind tightest of all.
- Parentheses group as usual.

## Calls
- A call is written `name(arg, ...)`. Built-in and user-defined functions use the same form.

## Indexing
- `xs[i]` reads an element. Indices start at **0**: the first element is `xs[0]`, the last is `xs[0 + len(xs) - 1]`.
- Indexing outside the valid range is a runtime error.

## Truthiness
- `kreedena`, `vedreebe`: as written. `none`: false.
- The integer 0 is false. An empty list or empty string is false. Any other integer is true.

## Integer arithmetic
- `/` on integers rounds toward zero: `-7 / 2` evaluates to -3.
- `%` takes the sign of the divisor: `-7 % 3` evaluates to 2, and `7 % -3` evaluates to -2.
- Division or modulo by zero is a runtime error. `+` also joins two strings.

## and / or
- `and` and `or` short-circuit. They return a boolean: `0 or 5` evaluates to true, `vedreebe and 0` evaluates to false.

## Built-in functions
- `brupu(x)`: number of items in a list or characters in a string.
- `trimai(xs, v)`: a new list with `v` added at the end.
- `briree(x)`: the printed form of `x` as a string.
- `kepen(x)`: prints `x` on its own line.

## Limits
- Programs get a bounded number of steps, a bounded call depth and bounded list and string sizes. Exceeding a limit (for example an infinite loop) is an error.

## Worked examples (executed when this manual was generated)

### Integer division and modulo
```
kepen(- 7 / 2) .
kepen(- 7 % 3) .
kepen(7 / - 2) .
kepen(7 % - 3) .
```
Output:
```
-3
2
-3
-2
```

### Operator precedence
```
kepen(2 + 3 * 4) .
kepen(1 == (0 + 1)) .
```
Output:
```
14
true
```

### Truthiness in conditions
```
glugi 0 poutrai
  kepen("zero is true") .
teevu supine poutrai
  kepen("zero is false") .
teevu
glugi[] poutrai
  kepen("empty list is true") .
teevu supine poutrai
  kepen("empty list is false") .
teevu
```
Output:
```
zero is false
empty list is false
```

### Indexing
```
xs = [10, 20, 30] .
kepen(xs[0]) .
kepen(xs[2]) .
```
Output:
```
10
30
```

### and / or
```
kepen(0 || 5) .
kepen(vedreebe && 0) .
```
Output:
```
true
false
```

### Functions, loops and calls
```
budru add(a, b) poutrai
  kezvai a + b .
teevu
acc = 0 .
i = 1 .
botu i <= 3 poutrai
  acc = acc + i .
  i = i + 1 .
teevu
kepen(acc) .
kepen(add(2, 3)) .
```
Output:
```
6
5
```

# Task: is prime
Define solve(n) returning true if the non-negative integer n is prime, otherwise false. 0 and 1 are not prime.

## Visible examples
- solve(4) returns false
- solve(0) returns false

## Answer format
Define a function named `solve` with the parameters described above. Reply with one fenced code block containing the complete program (top-level statements are allowed). Do not explain.

Attempt 1: failed, 0/7 tests, 0 in / 0 out tokens
Model answer
```python
def solve(n):
    if (n < 2):
        return False
    d = 2
    while ((d * d) <= n):
        if ((n % d) == 0):
            return False
        d = (d + 1)
    return True
```
Grader feedback
Your program has a syntax error: line 1: unexpected character ':'
PASS write.is_prime · Vezafra (seed 1) · oracle · 1 attempt(s)
Prompt (attempt 1)

Output:
```
zero is false
empty list is false
```

### Indexing
```
xs = [10, 20, 30] .
kepen(xs[0]) .
kepen(xs[2]) .
```
Output:
```
10
30
```

### and / or
```
kepen(0 || 5) .
kepen(vedreebe && 0) .
```
Output:
```
true
false
```

### Functions, loops and calls
```
budru add(a, b) poutrai
  kezvai a + b .
teevu
acc = 0 .
i = 1 .
botu i <= 3 poutrai
  acc = acc + i .
  i = i + 1 .
teevu

```

# Plakri: reference manual
# Plakri reference manual

Plakri is a small imperative language. A program is a list of statements. The grader calls the function `pegun solve(...)` with arguments and prints the value it returns.

## Words and values
- Function definition: `pegun`. Conditionals: `krivoulo` / `ribrumee`. Loops: `skedre`. Return: `gamee`.
- Booleans: `krufeebru` and `treplaigee`. Integers: `0`, `42` (negative numbers use the prefix negation operator `~`). Strings: double quotes, e.g. "hi".
- Lists are written `[a, b, c]`. Values are immutable: `append` returns a new list.
- A function that does not return a value yields `none`.
- Printed values: integers as digits, booleans as `true`/`false`, strings as-is, lists as `[a, b]`. Strings inside a list are in double quotes, e.g. `["a", 1]`.

## Statements
- Assignment: `name := expression`. The first assignment inside a function creates the variable.
- Blocks start with `meesete` and end with `buskee`. Statements inside a block follow the same rules.
- Statement endings: each simple statement ends with `$`.
- Identifiers are plain letters, digits and underscores (not starting with a digit).

## Operators and precedence
- Negation: `~x`. Logical not: `!x`. Both bind tighter than any binary operator.
- Binary operators, lowest binding first (same line = same precedence, grouped left to right):
1. `|`
2. `+`, `-`
3. `*`, `/`, `%`
4. `&`
5. `===`, `/=`, `<`, `=<`, `>`, `>=`
- Calls and indexing bind tightest of all.
- Parentheses group as usual.

## Calls
- A call is written `(arg, ...) name`, with the arguments first and the name last. Built-in and user-defined functions use the same form.

## Indexing
- `xs[i]` reads an element. Indices start at **1**: the first element is `xs[1]`, the last is `xs[1 + len(xs) - 1]`.
- Indexing outside the valid range is a runtime error.

## Truthiness
- `treplaigee`, `krufeebru`: as written. `none`: false.
- The integer 0 is false. An empty list or empty string is true. Any other integer is true.

## Integer arithmetic
- `/` on integers rounds toward negative infinity: `-7 / 2` evaluates to -4.
- `%` takes the sign of the divisor: `-7 % 3` evaluates to 2, and `7 % -3` evaluates to -2.
- Division or modulo by zero is a runtime error. `+` also joins two strings.

## and / or
- `and` and `or` short-circuit. They return the operand that decided the result: `0 or 5` evaluates to 5, `krufeebru and 0` evaluates to 0.

## Built-in functions
- `vopiro(x)`: number of items in a list or characters in a string.
- `zvuzukra(xs, v)`: a new list with `v` added at the end.
- `trudou(x)`: the printed form of `x` as a string.
- `saiskee(x)`: prints `x` on its own line.

## Limits
- Programs get a bounded number of steps, a bounded call depth and bounded list and string sizes. Exceeding a limit (for example an infinite loop) is an error.

## Worked examples (executed when this manual was generated)

### Integer division and modulo
```
(~ 7 / 2) saiskee $
(~ 7 % 3) saiskee $
(7 / ~ 2) saiskee $
(7 % ~ 3) saiskee $
```
Output:
```
-4
2
-4
-2
```

### Operator precedence
```
(2 + 3 * 4) saiskee $
(1 === (0 + 1)) saiskee $
```
Output:
```
14
true
```

### Truthiness in conditions
```
krivoulo 0 meesete
  ("zero is true") saiskee $
buskee ribrumee meesete
  ("zero is false") saiskee $
buskee
krivoulo[] meesete
  ("empty list is true") saiskee $
buskee ribrumee meesete
  ("empty list is false") saiskee $
buskee
```
Output:
```
zero is false
empty list is true
```

### Indexing
```
xs := [10, 20, 30] $
(xs[1]) saiskee $
(xs[3]) saiskee $
```
Output:
```
10
30
```

### and / or
```
(0 | 5) saiskee $
(krufeebru & 0) saiskee $
```
Output:
```
5
0
```

### Functions, loops and calls
```
pegun add(a, b) meesete
  gamee a + b $
buskee
acc := 0 $
i := 1 $
skedre i =< 3 meesete
  acc := acc + i $
  i := i + 1 $
buskee
(acc) saiskee $
((2, 3) add) saiskee $
```
Output:
```
6
5
```

Define solve(n) returning true if the non-negative integer n is prime, otherwise false. 0 and 1 are not prime.

```python
def solve(n):
    if (n < 2):
        return False
    d = 2
    while ((d * d) <= n):
        if ((n % d) == 0):
            return False
        d = (d + 1)
    return True
```

# Veleeni: reference manual
# Veleeni reference manual

Veleeni is a small imperative language. A program is a list of statements. The grader calls the function `romazva solve(...)` with arguments and prints the value it returns.

## Words and values
- Function definition: `romazva`. Conditionals: `kouzvi` / `traikeru`. Loops: `soureekra`. Return: `kromee`.
- Booleans: `pezvimo` and `plukrafee`. Integers: `0`, `42` (negative numbers use the prefix negation operator `-`). Strings: double quotes, e.g. "hi".
- Lists are written `[a, b, c]`. Values are immutable: `append` returns a new list.
- A function that does not return a value yields `none`.
- Printed values: integers as digits, booleans as `true`/`false`, strings as-is, lists as `[a, b]`. Strings inside a list are in double quotes, e.g. `["a", 1]`.

## Statements
- Assignment: `name = expression`. The first assignment inside a function creates the variable.
- Blocks start with `{` and end with `}`. Statements inside a block follow the same rules.
- Statement endings: a statement ends at the end of its line.
- In a newline-ended language a block opener must be on the same line as its statement, and `traikeru` must be on the same line as the `}` that closes the `if` block.
- Identifiers are plain letters, digits and underscores (not starting with a digit).

## Operators and precedence
- Negation: `-x`. Logical not: `fudaix`. Both bind tighter than any binary operator.
- Binary operators, lowest binding first (same line = same precedence, grouped left to right):
1. `||`
2. `&`
3. `===`, `!=`, `<`, `=<`, `>`, `=>`
4. `+`, `-`
5. `*`, `/`, `%`
- Calls and indexing bind tightest of all.
- Parentheses group as usual.

## Calls
- A call is written `(arg, ...) name`, with the arguments first and the name last. Built-in and user-defined functions use the same form.

## Indexing
- `xs[i]` reads an element. Indices start at **0**: the first element is `xs[0]`, the last is `xs[0 + len(xs) - 1]`.
- Indexing outside the valid range is a runtime error.

## Truthiness
- `plukrafee`, `pezvimo`: as written. `none`: false.
- The integer 0 is true. An empty list or empty string is false. Any other integer is true.

## Integer arithmetic
- `/` on integers rounds toward negative infinity: `-7 / 2` evaluates to -4.
- `%` takes the sign of the dividend: `-7 % 3` evaluates to -1, and `7 % -3` evaluates to 1.
- Division or modulo by zero is a runtime error. `+` also joins two strings.

## and / or
- `and` and `or` short-circuit. They return the operand that decided the result: `0 or 5` evaluates to 0, `pezvimo and 0` evaluates to 0.

## Built-in functions
- `kraizo(x)`: number of items in a list or characters in a string.
- `brale(xs, v)`: a new list with `v` added at the end.
- `dikuki(x)`: the printed form of `x` as a string.
- `mutre(x)`: prints `x` on its own line.

## Limits
- Programs get a bounded number of steps, a bounded call depth and bounded list and string sizes. Exceeding a limit (for example an infinite loop) is an error.

## Worked examples (executed when this manual was generated)

### Integer division and modulo
```

(- 7 % 3) mutre

(7 % - 3) mutre

```

```

```

```

```

### Functions, loops and calls

```
  kromee a + b
}
acc = 0
i = 1

  acc = acc + i
  i = i + 1
}
(acc) mutre
((2, 3) add) mutre
```

- `and` and `or` short-circuit. They return a boolean: `0 or 5` evaluates to true, `vedreebe and 0` evaluates to false.

## Built-in functions
- `brupu(x)`: number of items in a list or characters in a string.
- `trimai(xs, v)`: a new list with `v` added at the end.
- `briree(x)`: the printed form of `x` as a string.
- `kepen(x)`: prints `x` on its own line.

## Limits
- Programs get a bounded number of steps, a bounded call depth and bounded list and string sizes. Exceeding a limit (for example an infinite loop) is an error.

# Vezafra: reference manual
# Vezafra reference manual

Vezafra is a small imperative language. A program is a list of statements. The grader calls the function `budru solve(...)` with arguments and prints the value it returns.

## Words and values
- Function definition: `budru`. Conditionals: `glugi` / `supine`. Loops: `botu`. Return: `kezvai`.
- Booleans: `vedreebe` and `kreedena`. Integers: `0`, `42` (negative numbers use the prefix negation operator `-`). Strings: double quotes, e.g. "hi".
- Lists are written `[a, b, c]`. Values are immutable: `append` returns a new list.
- A function that does not return a value yields `none`.
- Printed values: integers as digits, booleans as `true`/`false`, strings as-is, lists as `[a, b]`. Strings inside a list are in double quotes, e.g. `["a", 1]`.

## Statements
- Assignment: `name = expression`. The first assignment inside a function creates the variable.
- Blocks start with `poutrai` and end with `teevu`. Statements inside a block follow the same rules.
- Statement endings: each simple statement ends with `.`.
- Identifiers are plain letters, digits and underscores (not starting with a digit).

## Operators and precedence
- Negation: `-x`. Logical not: `!x`. Both bind tighter than any binary operator.
- Binary operators, lowest binding first (same line = same precedence, grouped left to right):
1. `||`
2. `+`, `-`
3. `&&`
4. `==`, `!=`, `<`, `<=`, `>`, `>=`
5. `*`, `/`, `%`
- Calls and indexing bind tightest of all.
- Parentheses group as usual.

## Calls
- A call is written `name(arg, ...)`. Built-in and user-defined functions use the same form.

## Indexing
- `xs[i]` reads an element. Indices start at **0**: the first element is `xs[0]`, the last is `xs[0 + len(xs) - 1]`.
- Indexing outside the valid range is a runtime error.

## Truthiness
- `kreedena`, `vedreebe`: as written. `none`: false.
- The integer 0 is false. An empty list or empty string is false. Any other integer is true.

## Integer arithmetic
- `/` on integers rounds toward zero: `-7 / 2` evaluates to -3.
- `%` takes the sign of the divisor: `-7 % 3` evaluates to 2, and `7 % -3` evaluates to -2.
- Division or modulo by zero is a runtime error. `+` also joins two strings.

## and / or
- `and` and `or` short-circuit. They return a boolean: `0 or 5` evaluates to true, `vedreebe and 0` evaluates to false.

## Built-in functions
- `brupu(x)`: number of items in a list or characters in a string.
- `trimai(xs, v)`: a new list with `v` added at the end.
- `briree(x)`: the printed form of `x` as a string.
- `kepen(x)`: prints `x` on its own line.

## Limits
- Programs get a bounded number of steps, a bounded call depth and bounded list and string sizes. Exceeding a limit (for example an infinite loop) is an error.

## Worked examples (executed when this manual was generated)

# Plakri: reference manual
# Plakri reference manual

Plakri is a small imperative language. A program is a list of statements. The grader calls the function `pegun solve(...)` with arguments and prints the value it returns.

## Words and values
- Function definition: `pegun`. Conditionals: `krivoulo` / `ribrumee`. Loops: `skedre`. Return: `gamee`.
- Booleans: `krufeebru` and `treplaigee`. Integers: `0`, `42` (negative numbers use the prefix negation operator `~`). Strings: double quotes, e.g. "hi".
- Lists are written `[a, b, c]`. Values are immutable: `append` returns a new list.
- A function that does not return a value yields `none`.
- Printed values: integers as digits, booleans as `true`/`false`, strings as-is, lists as `[a, b]`. Strings inside a list are in double quotes, e.g. `["a", 1]`.

## Statements
- Assignment: `name := expression`. The first assignment inside a function creates the variable.
- Blocks start with `meesete` and end with `buskee`. Statements inside a block follow the same rules.
- Statement endings: each simple statement ends with `$`.
- Identifiers are plain letters, digits and underscores (not starting with a digit).

## Operators and precedence
- Negation: `~x`. Logical not: `!x`. Both bind tighter than any binary operator.
- Binary operators, lowest binding first (same line = same precedence, grouped left to right):
1. `|`
2. `+`, `-`
3. `*`, `/`, `%`
4. `&`
5. `===`, `/=`, `<`, `=<`, `>`, `>=`
- Calls and indexing bind tightest of all.
- Parentheses group as usual.

## Calls
- A call is written `(arg, ...) name`, with the arguments first and the name last. Built-in and user-defined functions use the same form.

## Indexing
- `xs[i]` reads an element. Indices start at **1**: the first element is `xs[1]`, the last is `xs[1 + len(xs) - 1]`.
- Indexing outside the valid range is a runtime error.

## Truthiness
- `treplaigee`, `krufeebru`: as written. `none`: false.
- The integer 0 is false. An empty list or empty string is true. Any other integer is true.

## Integer arithmetic
- `/` on integers rounds toward negative infinity: `-7 / 2` evaluates to -4.
- `%` takes the sign of the divisor: `-7 % 3` evaluates to 2, and `7 % -3` evaluates to -2.
- Division or modulo by zero is a runtime error. `+` also joins two strings.

## and / or
- `and` and `or` short-circuit. They return the operand that decided the result: `0 or 5` evaluates to 5, `krufeebru and 0` evaluates to 0.

## Built-in functions
- `vopiro(x)`: number of items in a list or characters in a string.
- `zvuzukra(xs, v)`: a new list with `v` added at the end.
- `trudou(x)`: the printed form of `x` as a string.
- `saiskee(x)`: prints `x` on its own line.

## Limits
- Programs get a bounded number of steps, a bounded call depth and bounded list and string sizes. Exceeding a limit (for example an infinite loop) is an error.

## Worked examples (executed when this manual was generated)

### Integer division and modulo
```
(~ 7 / 2) saiskee $
(~ 7 % 3) saiskee $
(7 / ~ 2) saiskee $
(7 % ~ 3) saiskee $
```
Output:
```
-4
2
-4
-2
```

### Operator precedence
```
(2 + 3 * 4) saiskee $
(1 === (0 + 1)) saiskee $
```
Output:
```
14
true
```

### Truthiness in conditions
```
krivoulo 0 meesete
  ("zero is true") saiskee $
buskee ribrumee meesete
  ("zero is false") saiskee $
buskee
krivoulo[] meesete
  ("empty list is true") saiskee $
buskee ribrumee meesete
  ("empty list is false") saiskee $
buskee
```
Output:
```
zero is false
empty list is true
```

### Indexing
```
xs := [10, 20, 30] $
(xs[1]) saiskee $
(xs[3]) saiskee $
```
Output:
```
10
30
```

### and / or
```
(0 | 5) saiskee $
(krufeebru & 0) saiskee $
```
Output:
```
5
0
```

### Functions, loops and calls
```
pegun add(a, b) meesete
  gamee a + b $
buskee
acc := 0 $
i := 1 $
skedre i =< 3 meesete
  acc := acc + i $
  i := i + 1 $
buskee
(acc) saiskee $
((2, 3) add) saiskee $
```
Output:
```
6
5
```

# Veleeni: reference manual
# Veleeni reference manual

Veleeni is a small imperative language. A program is a list of statements. The grader calls the function `romazva solve(...)` with arguments and prints the value it returns.

## Words and values
- Function definition: `romazva`. Conditionals: `kouzvi` / `traikeru`. Loops: `soureekra`. Return: `kromee`.
- Booleans: `pezvimo` and `plukrafee`. Integers: `0`, `42` (negative numbers use the prefix negation operator `-`). Strings: double quotes, e.g. "hi".
- Lists are written `[a, b, c]`. Values are immutable: `append` returns a new list.
- A function that does not return a value yields `none`.
- Printed values: integers as digits, booleans as `true`/`false`, strings as-is, lists as `[a, b]`. Strings inside a list are in double quotes, e.g. `["a", 1]`.

## Statements
- Assignment: `name = expression`. The first assignment inside a function creates the variable.
- Blocks start with `{` and end with `}`. Statements inside a block follow the same rules.
- Statement endings: a statement ends at the end of its line.
- In a newline-ended language a block opener must be on the same line as its statement, and `traikeru` must be on the same line as the `}` that closes the `if` block.
- Identifiers are plain letters, digits and underscores (not starting with a digit).

## Operators and precedence
- Negation: `-x`. Logical not: `fudaix`. Both bind tighter than any binary operator.
- Binary operators, lowest binding first (same line = same precedence, grouped left to right):
1. `||`
2. `&`
3. `===`, `!=`, `<`, `=<`, `>`, `=>`
4. `+`, `-`
5. `*`, `/`, `%`
- Calls and indexing bind tightest of all.
- Parentheses group as usual.

## Calls
- A call is written `(arg, ...) name`, with the arguments first and the name last. Built-in and user-defined functions use the same form.

## Indexing
- `xs[i]` reads an element. Indices start at **0**: the first element is `xs[0]`, the last is `xs[0 + len(xs) - 1]`.
- Indexing outside the valid range is a runtime error.

## Truthiness
- `plukrafee`, `pezvimo`: as written. `none`: false.
- The integer 0 is true. An empty list or empty string is false. Any other integer is true.

## Integer arithmetic
- `/` on integers rounds toward negative infinity: `-7 / 2` evaluates to -4.
- `%` takes the sign of the dividend: `-7 % 3` evaluates to -1, and `7 % -3` evaluates to 1.
- Division or modulo by zero is a runtime error. `+` also joins two strings.

## and / or
- `and` and `or` short-circuit. They return the operand that decided the result: `0 or 5` evaluates to 0, `pezvimo and 0` evaluates to 0.

## Built-in functions
- `kraizo(x)`: number of items in a list or characters in a string.
- `brale(xs, v)`: a new list with `v` added at the end.
- `dikuki(x)`: the printed form of `x` as a string.
- `mutre(x)`: prints `x` on its own line.

## Limits
- Programs get a bounded number of steps, a bounded call depth and bounded list and string sizes. Exceeding a limit (for example an infinite loop) is an error.

## Worked examples (executed when this manual was generated)

### Integer division and modulo
```

(- 7 % 3) mutre
(7 / - 2) mutre
(7 % - 3) mutre
```

```
  ("zero is true") mutre
} traikeru {
  ("zero is false") mutre

```

```
(xs[0]) mutre
(xs[2]) mutre
```

```
10
30
```

### and / or
```
(0 || 5) mutre
(pezvimo & 0) mutre
```
Output:
```
0
0
```

### Functions, loops and calls

```
  kromee a + b
}

i = 1

  acc = acc + i
  i = i + 1
}
(acc) mutre
((2, 3) add) mutre
```

```

5
```

- Negation: `-x`. Logical not: `fudaix`. Both bind tighter than any binary operator.
- Binary operators, lowest binding first (same line = same precedence, grouped left to right):
1. `||`
2. `&`
3. `===`, `!=`, `<`, `=<`, `>`, `=>`
4. `+`, `-`
5. `*`, `/`, `%`
- Calls and indexing bind tightest of all.
- Parentheses group as usual.

## Calls
- A call is written `(arg, ...) name`, with the arguments first and the name last. Built-in and user-defined functions use the same form.

## Indexing
- `xs[i]` reads an element. Indices start at **0**: the first element is `xs[0]`, the last is `xs[0 + len(xs) - 1]`.
- Indexing outside the valid range is a runtime error.

## Truthiness
- `plukrafee`, `pezvimo`: as written. `none`: false.
- The integer 0 is true. An empty list or empty string is false. Any other integer is true.

## Integer arithmetic
- `/` on integers rounds toward negative infinity: `-7 / 2` evaluates to -4.
- `%` takes the sign of the dividend: `-7 % 3` evaluates to -1, and `7 % -3` evaluates to 1.
- Division or modulo by zero is a runtime error. `+` also joins two strings.

## and / or
- `and` and `or` short-circuit. They return the operand that decided the result: `0 or 5` evaluates to 0, `pezvimo and 0` evaluates to 0.

## Built-in functions
- `kraizo(x)`: number of items in a list or characters in a string.
- `brale(xs, v)`: a new list with `v` added at the end.
- `dikuki(x)`: the printed form of `x` as a string.
- `mutre(x)`: prints `x` on its own line.

## Limits
- Programs get a bounded number of steps, a bounded call depth and bounded list and string sizes. Exceeding a limit (for example an infinite loop) is an error.

## Worked examples (executed when this manual was generated)

### Integer division and modulo
```
(- 7 / 2) mutre
(- 7 % 3) mutre
(7 / - 2) mutre
(7 % - 3) mutre
```
Output:
```
-4
-1
-4
1
```

### Operator precedence
```
(2 + 3 * 4) mutre
(1 === 0 + 1) mutre
```
Output:
```
14
true
```

### Truthiness in conditions
```
kouzvi 0 {
  ("zero is true") mutre
} traikeru {
  ("zero is false") mutre
}
kouzvi[] {
  ("empty list is true") mutre
} traikeru {
  ("empty list is false") mutre
}
```
Output:
```
zero is true
empty list is false
```

### Indexing
```
xs = [10, 20, 30]
(xs[0]) mutre
(xs[2]) mutre
```
Output:
```

30
```

### and / or
```
(0 || 5) mutre
(pezvimo & 0) mutre
```
Output:
```
0
0
```

### Functions, loops and calls
```
romazva add(a, b) {
  kromee a + b
}
acc = 0
i = 1
soureekra i =< 3 {
  acc = acc + i
  i = i + 1
}
(acc) mutre
((2, 3) add) mutre
```
Output:

# Vezafra: reference manual
# Vezafra reference manual

Vezafra is a small imperative language. A program is a list of statements. The grader calls the function `budru solve(...)` with arguments and prints the value it returns.

## Words and values
- Function definition: `budru`. Conditionals: `glugi` / `supine`. Loops: `botu`. Return: `kezvai`.
- Booleans: `vedreebe` and `kreedena`. Integers: `0`, `42` (negative numbers use the prefix negation operator `-`). Strings: double quotes, e.g. "hi".
- Lists are written `[a, b, c]`. Values are immutable: `append` returns a new list.
- A function that does not return a value yields `none`.
- Printed values: integers as digits, booleans as `true`/`false`, strings as-is, lists as `[a, b]`. Strings inside a list are in double quotes, e.g. `["a", 1]`.

## Statements
- Assignment: `name = expression`. The first assignment inside a function creates the variable.
- Blocks start with `poutrai` and end with `teevu`. Statements inside a block follow the same rules.
- Statement endings: each simple statement ends with `.`.
- Identifiers are plain letters, digits and underscores (not starting with a digit).

## Operators and precedence
- Negation: `-x`. Logical not: `!x`. Both bind tighter than any binary operator.
- Binary operators, lowest binding first (same line = same precedence, grouped left to right):
1. `||`
2. `+`, `-`
3. `&&`
4. `==`, `!=`, `<`, `<=`, `>`, `>=`
5. `*`, `/`, `%`
- Calls and indexing bind tightest of all.
- Parentheses group as usual.

## Calls
- A call is written `name(arg, ...)`. Built-in and user-defined functions use the same form.

## Indexing
- `xs[i]` reads an element. Indices start at **0**: the first element is `xs[0]`, the last is `xs[0 + len(xs) - 1]`.
- Indexing outside the valid range is a runtime error.

## Truthiness
- `kreedena`, `vedreebe`: as written. `none`: false.
- The integer 0 is false. An empty list or empty string is false. Any other integer is true.

## Integer arithmetic
- `/` on integers rounds toward zero: `-7 / 2` evaluates to -3.
- `%` takes the sign of the divisor: `-7 % 3` evaluates to 2, and `7 % -3` evaluates to -2.
- Division or modulo by zero is a runtime error. `+` also joins two strings.

## and / or
- `and` and `or` short-circuit. They return a boolean: `0 or 5` evaluates to true, `vedreebe and 0` evaluates to false.

## Built-in functions
- `brupu(x)`: number of items in a list or characters in a string.
- `trimai(xs, v)`: a new list with `v` added at the end.
- `briree(x)`: the printed form of `x` as a string.
- `kepen(x)`: prints `x` on its own line.

## Limits
- Programs get a bounded number of steps, a bounded call depth and bounded list and string sizes. Exceeding a limit (for example an infinite loop) is an error.

## Worked examples (executed when this manual was generated)

### Integer division and modulo
```

kepen(7 % - 3) .
```
Output:
```

2
-3
-2
```

### Operator precedence
```

```
Output:
```
14
true
```

### Truthiness in conditions
```

  kepen("zero is true") .
teevu supine poutrai
  kepen("zero is false") .
teevu

  kepen("empty list is true") .
teevu supine poutrai
  kepen("empty list is false") .
teevu
```

```
zero is false
empty list is false
```

### Indexing
```

kepen(xs[2]) .
```

```

30

```
Output:
```
true
false
```

### Functions, loops and calls
```

  kezvai a + b .
teevu
acc = 0 .
i = 1 .
botu i <= 3 poutrai
  acc = acc + i .
  i = i + 1 .
teevu

kepen(add(2, 3)) .
```
Output:
```
6
5
```

# Plakri: reference manual
# Plakri reference manual

Plakri is a small imperative language. A program is a list of statements. The grader calls the function `pegun solve(...)` with arguments and prints the value it returns.

## Words and values
- Function definition: `pegun`. Conditionals: `krivoulo` / `ribrumee`. Loops: `skedre`. Return: `gamee`.
- Booleans: `krufeebru` and `treplaigee`. Integers: `0`, `42` (negative numbers use the prefix negation operator `~`). Strings: double quotes, e.g. "hi".
- Lists are written `[a, b, c]`. Values are immutable: `append` returns a new list.
- A function that does not return a value yields `none`.
- Printed values: integers as digits, booleans as `true`/`false`, strings as-is, lists as `[a, b]`. Strings inside a list are in double quotes, e.g. `["a", 1]`.

## Statements
- Assignment: `name := expression`. The first assignment inside a function creates the variable.
- Blocks start with `meesete` and end with `buskee`. Statements inside a block follow the same rules.
- Statement endings: each simple statement ends with `$`.
- Identifiers are plain letters, digits and underscores (not starting with a digit).

## Operators and precedence
- Negation: `~x`. Logical not: `!x`. Both bind tighter than any binary operator.
- Binary operators, lowest binding first (same line = same precedence, grouped left to right):
1. `|`
2. `+`, `-`
3. `*`, `/`, `%`
4. `&`
5. `===`, `/=`, `<`, `=<`, `>`, `>=`
- Calls and indexing bind tightest of all.
- Parentheses group as usual.

## Calls
- A call is written `(arg, ...) name`, with the arguments first and the name last. Built-in and user-defined functions use the same form.

## Indexing
- `xs[i]` reads an element. Indices start at **1**: the first element is `xs[1]`, the last is `xs[1 + len(xs) - 1]`.
- Indexing outside the valid range is a runtime error.

## Truthiness
- `treplaigee`, `krufeebru`: as written. `none`: false.
- The integer 0 is false. An empty list or empty string is true. Any other integer is true.

## Integer arithmetic
- `/` on integers rounds toward negative infinity: `-7 / 2` evaluates to -4.
- `%` takes the sign of the divisor: `-7 % 3` evaluates to 2, and `7 % -3` evaluates to -2.
- Division or modulo by zero is a runtime error. `+` also joins two strings.

## and / or
- `and` and `or` short-circuit. They return the operand that decided the result: `0 or 5` evaluates to 5, `krufeebru and 0` evaluates to 0.

## Built-in functions
- `vopiro(x)`: number of items in a list or characters in a string.
- `zvuzukra(xs, v)`: a new list with `v` added at the end.
- `trudou(x)`: the printed form of `x` as a string.
- `saiskee(x)`: prints `x` on its own line.

## Limits
- Programs get a bounded number of steps, a bounded call depth and bounded list and string sizes. Exceeding a limit (for example an infinite loop) is an error.

## Worked examples (executed when this manual was generated)

### Integer division and modulo
```
(~ 7 / 2) saiskee $
(~ 7 % 3) saiskee $
(7 / ~ 2) saiskee $
(7 % ~ 3) saiskee $
```
Output:
```
-4
2
-4
-2
```

### Operator precedence
```
(2 + 3 * 4) saiskee $
(1 === (0 + 1)) saiskee $
```
Output:
```
14
true
```

### Truthiness in conditions
```
krivoulo 0 meesete
  ("zero is true") saiskee $
buskee ribrumee meesete
  ("zero is false") saiskee $
buskee
krivoulo[] meesete
  ("empty list is true") saiskee $
buskee ribrumee meesete
  ("empty list is false") saiskee $
buskee
```
Output:
```
zero is false
empty list is true
```

### Indexing
```
xs := [10, 20, 30] $
(xs[1]) saiskee $
(xs[3]) saiskee $
```
Output:
```
10
30
```

### and / or
```
(0 | 5) saiskee $
(krufeebru & 0) saiskee $
```
Output:
```
5
0
```

### Functions, loops and calls
```
pegun add(a, b) meesete
  gamee a + b $
buskee
acc := 0 $
i := 1 $
skedre i =< 3 meesete
  acc := acc + i $
  i := i + 1 $
buskee
(acc) saiskee $
((2, 3) add) saiskee $
```
Output:
```
6
5
```

## Words and values
- Function definition: `romazva`. Conditionals: `kouzvi` / `traikeru`. Loops: `soureekra`. Return: `kromee`.
- Booleans: `pezvimo` and `plukrafee`. Integers: `0`, `42` (negative numbers use the prefix negation operator `-`). Strings: double quotes, e.g. "hi".
- Lists are written `[a, b, c]`. Values are immutable: `append` returns a new list.
- A function that does not return a value yields `none`.
- Printed values: integers as digits, booleans as `true`/`false`, strings as-is, lists as `[a, b]`. Strings inside a list are in double quotes, e.g. `["a", 1]`.

## Statements
- Assignment: `name = expression`. The first assignment inside a function creates the variable.
- Blocks start with `{` and end with `}`. Statements inside a block follow the same rules.
- Statement endings: a statement ends at the end of its line.
- In a newline-ended language a block opener must be on the same line as its statement, and `traikeru` must be on the same line as the `}` that closes the `if` block.
- Identifiers are plain letters, digits and underscores (not starting with a digit).

## Operators and precedence
- Negation: `-x`. Logical not: `fudaix`. Both bind tighter than any binary operator.
- Binary operators, lowest binding first (same line = same precedence, grouped left to right):
1. `||`
2. `&`
3. `===`, `!=`, `<`, `=<`, `>`, `=>`
4. `+`, `-`
5. `*`, `/`, `%`
- Calls and indexing bind tightest of all.
- Parentheses group as usual.

## Calls
- A call is written `(arg, ...) name`, with the arguments first and the name last. Built-in and user-defined functions use the same form.

## Indexing
- `xs[i]` reads an element. Indices start at **0**: the first element is `xs[0]`, the last is `xs[0 + len(xs) - 1]`.
- Indexing outside the valid range is a runtime error.

## Truthiness
- `plukrafee`, `pezvimo`: as written. `none`: false.
- The integer 0 is true. An empty list or empty string is false. Any other integer is true.

## Integer arithmetic
- `/` on integers rounds toward negative infinity: `-7 / 2` evaluates to -4.
- `%` takes the sign of the dividend: `-7 % 3` evaluates to -1, and `7 % -3` evaluates to 1.
- Division or modulo by zero is a runtime error. `+` also joins two strings.

## and / or
- `and` and `or` short-circuit. They return the operand that decided the result: `0 or 5` evaluates to 0, `pezvimo and 0` evaluates to 0.

## Built-in functions
- `kraizo(x)`: number of items in a list or characters in a string.
- `brale(xs, v)`: a new list with `v` added at the end.
- `dikuki(x)`: the printed form of `x` as a string.
- `mutre(x)`: prints `x` on its own line.

## Limits
- Programs get a bounded number of steps, a bounded call depth and bounded list and string sizes. Exceeding a limit (for example an infinite loop) is an error.

## Worked examples (executed when this manual was generated)

### Integer division and modulo
```
(- 7 / 2) mutre
(- 7 % 3) mutre
(7 / - 2) mutre
(7 % - 3) mutre
```
Output:
```
-4
-1
-4
1
```

### Operator precedence
```
(2 + 3 * 4) mutre
(1 === 0 + 1) mutre
```
Output:
```
14
true
```

### Truthiness in conditions
```
kouzvi 0 {
  ("zero is true") mutre
} traikeru {
  ("zero is false") mutre
}
kouzvi[] {
  ("empty list is true") mutre
} traikeru {
  ("empty list is false") mutre
}
```
Output:
```
zero is true
empty list is false
```

### Indexing
```
xs = [10, 20, 30]
(xs[0]) mutre
(xs[2]) mutre
```
Output:
```
10
30
```

### and / or
```
(0 || 5) mutre
(pezvimo & 0) mutre
```
Output:
```
0
0
```

### Functions, loops and calls
```
romazva add(a, b) {
  kromee a + b
}
acc = 0
i = 1
soureekra i =< 3 {
  acc = acc + i
  i = i + 1
}
(acc) mutre
((2, 3) add) mutre
```
Output:
```
6
5
```

```python
def solve(xs):
    out = []
    i = (len(xs) - 1)
    while (i >= 0):
        out = out + [xs[i]]
        i = (i - 1)
```

# Vezafra: reference manual
# Vezafra reference manual

Vezafra is a small imperative language. A program is a list of statements. The grader calls the function `budru solve(...)` with arguments and prints the value it returns.

## Words and values
- Function definition: `budru`. Conditionals: `glugi` / `supine`. Loops: `botu`. Return: `kezvai`.
- Booleans: `vedreebe` and `kreedena`. Integers: `0`, `42` (negative numbers use the prefix negation operator `-`). Strings: double quotes, e.g. "hi".
- Lists are written `[a, b, c]`. Values are immutable: `append` returns a new list.
- A function that does not return a value yields `none`.
- Printed values: integers as digits, booleans as `true`/`false`, strings as-is, lists as `[a, b]`. Strings inside a list are in double quotes, e.g. `["a", 1]`.

## Statements
- Assignment: `name = expression`. The first assignment inside a function creates the variable.
- Blocks start with `poutrai` and end with `teevu`. Statements inside a block follow the same rules.
- Statement endings: each simple statement ends with `.`.
- Identifiers are plain letters, digits and underscores (not starting with a digit).

## Operators and precedence
- Negation: `-x`. Logical not: `!x`. Both bind tighter than any binary operator.
- Binary operators, lowest binding first (same line = same precedence, grouped left to right):
1. `||`
2. `+`, `-`
3. `&&`
4. `==`, `!=`, `<`, `<=`, `>`, `>=`
5. `*`, `/`, `%`
- Calls and indexing bind tightest of all.
- Parentheses group as usual.

## Calls
- A call is written `name(arg, ...)`. Built-in and user-defined functions use the same form.

## Indexing
- `xs[i]` reads an element. Indices start at **0**: the first element is `xs[0]`, the last is `xs[0 + len(xs) - 1]`.
- Indexing outside the valid range is a runtime error.

## Truthiness
- `kreedena`, `vedreebe`: as written. `none`: false.
- The integer 0 is false. An empty list or empty string is false. Any other integer is true.

## Integer arithmetic
- `/` on integers rounds toward zero: `-7 / 2` evaluates to -3.
- `%` takes the sign of the divisor: `-7 % 3` evaluates to 2, and `7 % -3` evaluates to -2.
- Division or modulo by zero is a runtime error. `+` also joins two strings.

## and / or
- `and` and `or` short-circuit. They return a boolean: `0 or 5` evaluates to true, `vedreebe and 0` evaluates to false.

## Built-in functions
- `brupu(x)`: number of items in a list or characters in a string.

# Plakri: reference manual
# Plakri reference manual

Plakri is a small imperative language. A program is a list of statements. The grader calls the function `pegun solve(...)` with arguments and prints the value it returns.

## Words and values
- Function definition: `pegun`. Conditionals: `krivoulo` / `ribrumee`. Loops: `skedre`. Return: `gamee`.
- Booleans: `krufeebru` and `treplaigee`. Integers: `0`, `42` (negative numbers use the prefix negation operator `~`). Strings: double quotes, e.g. "hi".
- Lists are written `[a, b, c]`. Values are immutable: `append` returns a new list.
- A function that does not return a value yields `none`.
- Printed values: integers as digits, booleans as `true`/`false`, strings as-is, lists as `[a, b]`. Strings inside a list are in double quotes, e.g. `["a", 1]`.

## Statements
- Assignment: `name := expression`. The first assignment inside a function creates the variable.
- Blocks start with `meesete` and end with `buskee`. Statements inside a block follow the same rules.
- Statement endings: each simple statement ends with `$`.
- Identifiers are plain letters, digits and underscores (not starting with a digit).

## Operators and precedence
- Negation: `~x`. Logical not: `!x`. Both bind tighter than any binary operator.
- Binary operators, lowest binding first (same line = same precedence, grouped left to right):
1. `|`
2. `+`, `-`
3. `*`, `/`, `%`
4. `&`
5. `===`, `/=`, `<`, `=<`, `>`, `>=`
- Calls and indexing bind tightest of all.
- Parentheses group as usual.

## Calls
- A call is written `(arg, ...) name`, with the arguments first and the name last. Built-in and user-defined functions use the same form.

## Indexing
- `xs[i]` reads an element. Indices start at **1**: the first element is `xs[1]`, the last is `xs[1 + len(xs) - 1]`.
- Indexing outside the valid range is a runtime error.

## Truthiness
- `treplaigee`, `krufeebru`: as written. `none`: false.
- The integer 0 is false. An empty list or empty string is true. Any other integer is true.

## Integer arithmetic
- `/` on integers rounds toward negative infinity: `-7 / 2` evaluates to -4.
- `%` takes the sign of the divisor: `-7 % 3` evaluates to 2, and `7 % -3` evaluates to -2.
- Division or modulo by zero is a runtime error. `+` also joins two strings.

## and / or
- `and` and `or` short-circuit. They return the operand that decided the result: `0 or 5` evaluates to 5, `krufeebru and 0` evaluates to 0.

## Built-in functions
- `vopiro(x)`: number of items in a list or characters in a string.
- `zvuzukra(xs, v)`: a new list with `v` added at the end.
- `trudou(x)`: the printed form of `x` as a string.
- `saiskee(x)`: prints `x` on its own line.

## Limits
- Programs get a bounded number of steps, a bounded call depth and bounded list and string sizes. Exceeding a limit (for example an infinite loop) is an error.

## Worked examples (executed when this manual was generated)

### Integer division and modulo
```
(~ 7 / 2) saiskee $
(~ 7 % 3) saiskee $
(7 / ~ 2) saiskee $
(7 % ~ 3) saiskee $
```
Output:
```
-4
2
-4
-2
```

### Operator precedence
```
(2 + 3 * 4) saiskee $
(1 === (0 + 1)) saiskee $
```
Output:
```
14
true
```

### Truthiness in conditions
```
krivoulo 0 meesete
  ("zero is true") saiskee $
buskee ribrumee meesete
  ("zero is false") saiskee $
buskee
krivoulo[] meesete
  ("empty list is true") saiskee $
buskee ribrumee meesete
  ("empty list is false") saiskee $
buskee
```
Output:
```
zero is false
empty list is true
```

### Indexing
```
xs := [10, 20, 30] $
(xs[1]) saiskee $
(xs[3]) saiskee $
```
Output:
```
10
30
```

### and / or
```
(0 | 5) saiskee $
(krufeebru & 0) saiskee $
```
Output:
```
5
0
```

### Functions, loops and calls
```
pegun add(a, b) meesete
  gamee a + b $
buskee
acc := 0 $
i := 1 $
skedre i =< 3 meesete
  acc := acc + i $
  i := i + 1 $
buskee
(acc) saiskee $
((2, 3) add) saiskee $
```
Output:
```
6
5
```

```python
def solve(n):
    acc = 0
    i = 1
    while (i <= n):
        acc = (acc + i)
        i = (i + 1)
    return acc
```

Prompt (attempt 1)
# Plakri: reference manual
# Plakri reference manual

Plakri is a small imperative language. A program is a list of statements. The grader calls the function `pegun solve(...)` with arguments and prints the value it returns.

## Words and values
- Function definition: `pegun`. Conditionals: `krivoulo` / `ribrumee`. Loops: `skedre`. Return: `gamee`.
- Booleans: `krufeebru` and `treplaigee`. Integers: `0`, `42` (negative numbers use the prefix negation operator `~`). Strings: double quotes, e.g. "hi".
- Lists are written `[a, b, c]`. Values are immutable: `append` returns a new list.
- A function that does not return a value yields `none`.
- Printed values: integers as digits, booleans as `true`/`false`, strings as-is, lists as `[a, b]`. Strings inside a list are in double quotes, e.g. `["a", 1]`.

## Statements
- Assignment: `name := expression`. The first assignment inside a function creates the variable.
- Blocks start with `meesete` and end with `buskee`. Statements inside a block follow the same rules.
- Statement endings: each simple statement ends with `$`.
- Identifiers are plain letters, digits and underscores (not starting with a digit).

## Operators and precedence
- Negation: `~x`. Logical not: `!x`. Both bind tighter than any binary operator.
- Binary operators, lowest binding first (same line = same precedence, grouped left to right):
1. `|`
2. `+`, `-`
3. `*`, `/`, `%`
4. `&`
5. `===`, `/=`, `<`, `=<`, `>`, `>=`
- Calls and indexing bind tightest of all.
- Parentheses group as usual.

## Calls
- A call is written `(arg, ...) name`, with the arguments first and the name last. Built-in and user-defined functions use the same form.

## Indexing
- `xs[i]` reads an element. Indices start at **1**: the first element is `xs[1]`, the last is `xs[1 + len(xs) - 1]`.
- Indexing outside the valid range is a runtime error.

## Truthiness
- `treplaigee`, `krufeebru`: as written. `none`: false.
- The integer 0 is false. An empty list or empty string is true. Any other integer is true.

## Integer arithmetic
- `/` on integers rounds toward negative infinity: `-7 / 2` evaluates to -4.
- `%` takes the sign of the divisor: `-7 % 3` evaluates to 2, and `7 % -3` evaluates to -2.
- Division or modulo by zero is a runtime error. `+` also joins two strings.

## and / or
- `and` and `or` short-circuit. They return the operand that decided the result: `0 or 5` evaluates to 5, `krufeebru and 0` evaluates to 0.

## Built-in functions
- `vopiro(x)`: number of items in a list or characters in a string.
- `zvuzukra(xs, v)`: a new list with `v` added at the end.
- `trudou(x)`: the printed form of `x` as a string.
- `saiskee(x)`: prints `x` on its own line.

## Limits
- Programs get a bounded number of steps, a bounded call depth and bounded list and string sizes. Exceeding a limit (for example an infinite loop) is an error.

## Worked examples (executed when this manual was generated)

### Integer division and modulo
```
(~ 7 / 2) saiskee $
(~ 7 % 3) saiskee $
(7 / ~ 2) saiskee $
(7 % ~ 3) saiskee $
```
Output:
```
-4
2
-4
-2
```

### Operator precedence
```
(2 + 3 * 4) saiskee $
(1 === (0 + 1)) saiskee $
```
Output:
```
14
true
```

### Truthiness in conditions
```
krivoulo 0 meesete
  ("zero is true") saiskee $
buskee ribrumee meesete
  ("zero is false") saiskee $
buskee
krivoulo[] meesete
  ("empty list is true") saiskee $
buskee ribrumee meesete
  ("empty list is false") saiskee $
buskee
```
Output:
```
zero is false
empty list is true
```

### Indexing
```
xs := [10, 20, 30] $
(xs[1]) saiskee $
(xs[3]) saiskee $
```
Output:
```
10
30
```

### and / or
```
(0 | 5) saiskee $
(krufeebru & 0) saiskee $
```
Output:
```
5
0
```

### Functions, loops and calls
```
pegun add(a, b) meesete
  gamee a + b $
buskee
acc := 0 $
i := 1 $
skedre i =< 3 meesete
  acc := acc + i $
  i := i + 1 $
buskee
(acc) saiskee $
((2, 3) add) saiskee $
```
Output:
```
6
5
```

# Veleeni: reference manual
# Veleeni reference manual

Veleeni is a small imperative language. A program is a list of statements. The grader calls the function `romazva solve(...)` with arguments and prints the value it returns.

## Words and values
- Function definition: `romazva`. Conditionals: `kouzvi` / `traikeru`. Loops: `soureekra`. Return: `kromee`.
- Booleans: `pezvimo` and `plukrafee`. Integers: `0`, `42` (negative numbers use the prefix negation operator `-`). Strings: double quotes, e.g. "hi".
- Lists are written `[a, b, c]`. Values are immutable: `append` returns a new list.
- A function that does not return a value yields `none`.
- Printed values: integers as digits, booleans as `true`/`false`, strings as-is, lists as `[a, b]`. Strings inside a list are in double quotes, e.g. `["a", 1]`.

## Statements
- Assignment: `name = expression`. The first assignment inside a function creates the variable.
- Blocks start with `{` and end with `}`. Statements inside a block follow the same rules.
- Statement endings: a statement ends at the end of its line.
- In a newline-ended language a block opener must be on the same line as its statement, and `traikeru` must be on the same line as the `}` that closes the `if` block.
- Identifiers are plain letters, digits and underscores (not starting with a digit).

## Operators and precedence
- Negation: `-x`. Logical not: `fudaix`. Both bind tighter than any binary operator.
- Binary operators, lowest binding first (same line = same precedence, grouped left to right):
1. `||`
2. `&`
3. `===`, `!=`, `<`, `=<`, `>`, `=>`
4. `+`, `-`
5. `*`, `/`, `%`
- Calls and indexing bind tightest of all.
- Parentheses group as usual.

## Calls
- A call is written `(arg, ...) name`, with the arguments first and the name last. Built-in and user-defined functions use the same form.

## Indexing
- `xs[i]` reads an element. Indices start at **0**: the first element is `xs[0]`, the last is `xs[0 + len(xs) - 1]`.
- Indexing outside the valid range is a runtime error.

## Truthiness
- `plukrafee`, `pezvimo`: as written. `none`: false.
- The integer 0 is true. An empty list or empty string is false. Any other integer is true.

## Integer arithmetic
- `/` on integers rounds toward negative infinity: `-7 / 2` evaluates to -4.
- `%` takes the sign of the dividend: `-7 % 3` evaluates to -1, and `7 % -3` evaluates to 1.
- Division or modulo by zero is a runtime error. `+` also joins two strings.

## and / or
- `and` and `or` short-circuit. They return the operand that decided the result: `0 or 5` evaluates to 0, `pezvimo and 0` evaluates to 0.

## Built-in functions
- `kraizo(x)`: number of items in a list or characters in a string.
- `brale(xs, v)`: a new list with `v` added at the end.
- `dikuki(x)`: the printed form of `x` as a string.
- `mutre(x)`: prints `x` on its own line.

## Limits
- Programs get a bounded number of steps, a bounded call depth and bounded list and string sizes. Exceeding a limit (for example an infinite loop) is an error.

## Worked examples (executed when this manual was generated)

### Integer division and modulo
```
(- 7 / 2) mutre
(- 7 % 3) mutre
(7 / - 2) mutre
(7 % - 3) mutre
```
Output:
```
-4
-1
-4
1
```

### Operator precedence
```
(2 + 3 * 4) mutre
(1 === 0 + 1) mutre
```
Output:
```
14
true
```

### Truthiness in conditions
```
kouzvi 0 {
  ("zero is true") mutre
} traikeru {
  ("zero is false") mutre
}
kouzvi[] {
  ("empty list is true") mutre
} traikeru {
  ("empty list is false") mutre
}
```
Output:
```
zero is true
empty list is false
```

### Indexing
```
xs = [10, 20, 30]
(xs[0]) mutre
(xs[2]) mutre
```
Output:
```
10
30
```

### and / or
```
(0 || 5) mutre
(pezvimo & 0) mutre
```
Output:
```
0
0
```

### Functions, loops and calls
```
romazva add(a, b) {
  kromee a + b
}
acc = 0
i = 1
soureekra i =< 3 {
  acc = acc + i
  i = i + 1
}
(acc) mutre
((2, 3) add) mutre
```
Output:
```
6
5
```
